I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you're not enjoying the things you used to, like choir or other hobbies, as much as you did before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Choir still means a lot to me… or it did—well, it does. It’s just hard to separate whether I’m not enjoying it or whether I’m just tired by the time I get there, you know?\n\nAnd hobbies… I still putter around the house. I was trying to sort some old music folders the other day and found a recipe card tucked in one—don’t ask me why, I’m always putting things in odd places. So I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy things anymore. I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in choir or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure I can say that clearly. Choir is still choir, you know? I’ve been doing it so long it’s almost part of the week, like taking the bins out or checking whether I left the porch light on.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m just not in the mood, but then I’ll get there and hear someone warming up and it feels familiar. Other times I start thinking about whether I remembered to bring the right folder, or if I need to pick up milk on the way home, and then I realize I haven’t really thought about whether I enjoyed it or not.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a straightforward way. It’s all a bit muddled.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling less pleasure when you are participating in choir or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve sat there in choir and thought, “Oh, I’m not enjoying this.” It’s more like… I’m doing it, and then my mind wanders to whether I turned the stove off or if I forgot someone’s birthday card.\n\nSometimes the singing feels nice, I suppose, but then someone will mention a hymn we used to do years ago and I start remembering all sorts of other things—like the old church hall with that awful coffee urn that always leaked. So I don’t know if it’s less pleasure exactly. I haven’t been measuring it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting less pleasure out of the things you do compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can compare it neatly, honestly. Before she passed… well, things were different, but I don’t always remember exactly how I felt doing things then versus now.\n\nSometimes I’ll be at choir and I’m more focused on whether I’m standing in the right place or if I brought my glasses—last week I had them on my head the whole time, which is silly—and then I think, “Was I enjoying that?” I’m not sure.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I know I’m getting less pleasure. I just haven’t really noticed it in that clear a way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad or empty most of the time exactly, but… ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself.\n\nI’ll be looking at the potluck list for church and suddenly I’m crying over who’s bringing coleslaw, which is ridiculous, really. And calls I used to make without thinking—just ordinary planning calls—I get all worked up about them now. I’ve even missed some church planning things I never would have missed before. I used to be the one reminding everyone else.\n\nSo… yes, something’s been off since then. It’s not always “empty,” maybe, but I’m not myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I still go to things, mostly. Choir, church bits and pieces… although I did miss that one meeting, but that was partly because I got the time mixed up, and then I couldn’t find the little notebook where I’d written it down. I’ve had that notebook for years, it’s got a blue cover—well, it used to be blue, now it’s sort of gray from being in my handbag.\n\nSo… lost interest? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought, “I don’t care about this anymore.” It’s more that I don’t always feel quite like myself when I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less interest in the things you do now compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t sat down and measured it against before, if that makes sense. I still turn up to things, or I try to—choir and church things and all that—though sometimes I’m a bit scattered about it.\n\nIt’s funny, I was looking for my choir folder the other day and found an old recipe card instead, my mother’s handwriting on it, and then I got completely sidetracked thinking I should make that casserole again. So… what was the question? Interest. Right.\n\nI don’t think I’ve clearly noticed having less interest, no. It’s more that I don’t always know how I feel while I’m doing things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “pulling away” sounds so deliberate, doesn’t it? Like I’m choosing not to answer the phone or not to see people. I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nI still talk to people, though sometimes I get mixed up about who I meant to call. My sister rang the other day and I was in the middle of sorting old church bulletins—why I kept so many, I couldn’t tell you—and then I forgot to call her back until later. But I don’t think I’m trying to keep away from anyone.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed pulling away, no. Not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social gatherings or events since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I still mean to go. I’ll have the date in my head and then the potluck list comes around, and I just start crying over whether we need coleslaw or rolls, which is ridiculous, isn’t it?\n\nSince my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t quite been myself. Things like church planning meetings, calls I used to make without thinking—now I get all worked up, and I’ve missed things I really wouldn’t have missed before. Not because I don’t care. I just… I don’t seem to manage it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself wanting to be alone more often since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like my quiet time anyway—always have, really. My husband used to say I could spend a whole afternoon rearranging a drawer and be perfectly content, though that’s not quite fair.\n\nSince she passed… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m home and I think, “Oh, I should call someone,” and then I end up watering the plants or looking for a bill I already paid. But I haven’t really noticed myself wanting to be alone more, not clearly. It’s more that things sort of get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy or look forward to activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I still like things when I get there, if that makes sense. Like choir—well, not quite the same lately, of course, but I wouldn’t say I don’t enjoy anything anymore.\n\nSometimes I think I’m looking forward to something, and then I get distracted by some little chore. Yesterday I found myself cleaning out a recipe box, and there were three different cards for banana bread—why anyone needs three, I don’t know. My mother’s handwriting was on one of them, and then I sat there longer than I meant to.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it’s that I no longer enjoy things. I just get a bit tangled up getting to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often, exactly. I mean, she comes to mind, sure—especially with choir things, or if I see something she would’ve had an opinion about. She had very particular opinions about tablecloths, of all things.\n\nBut I don’t sit there all day thinking about her, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… little reminders pop up, and then I get carried off for a bit. I’m not sure how much is normal, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like a part of you has died along with your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that sounds very dramatic when you put it that way. I wouldn’t say a part of me died, no. I miss her, of course, and there are these odd little gaps—like when I see something funny and think, oh, she’d appreciate that.\n\nBut “part of me died”… I’m not sure. It’s more like the room is arranged differently now, if that makes any sense. Like when you move a chair and keep bumping into where it used to be. My sister did that once with a coffee table, and I had a bruise for a week—anyway. No, I don’t think I’d put it that way exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like choir—well, not quite the same lately, of course, but I wouldn’t say I don’t enjoy anything anymore.\n\nSometimes I think I’m looking forward to something, and then I get distracted by some little chore. Yesterday I found myself cleaning out a recipe box, and there were three different cards for banana bread—why anyone needs three, I don’t know. My mother’s handwriting was on one of them, and then I sat there longer than I meant to.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it’s that I no longer enjoy things. I just get a bit tangled up getting to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often, exactly. I mean, she comes to mind, sure—especially with choir things, or if I see something she would’ve had an opinion about. She had very particular opinions about tablecloths, of all things.\n\nBut I don’t sit there all day thinking about her, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… little reminders pop up, and then I get carried off for a bit. I’m not sure how much is normal, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like a part of you has died along with your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that sounds very dramatic when you put it that way. I wouldn’t say a part of me died, no. I miss her, of course, and there are these odd little gaps—like when I see something funny and think, oh, she’d appreciate that.\n\nBut “part of me died”… I’m not sure. It’s more like the room is arranged differently now, if that makes any sense. Like when you move a chair and keep bumping into where it used to be. My sister did that once with a coffee table, and I had a bruise for a week—anyway. No, I don’t think I’d put it that way exactly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the sadness about your friend's passing is still as intense as it was initially?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to measure that, really. At first it was… well, everything felt very sharp, I suppose. Now it comes and goes more, maybe? Some days I think I’m doing all right, and then something small happens—like I’ll see a hymnal left open, or I’ll hear someone laugh in a way that’s almost like her, and there it is again.

But “as intense as initially”… I don’t know. It’s different, I think. Not gone. Just different. My neighbor once said grief is like weather, which I thought was a bit much at the time, but I suppose there’s something to it. Some days it’s just cloudy, and other days you didn’t bring an umbrella.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. We used to sit together and go over the music, and she always knew who was bringing what to the potlucks—now I look at those lists and I just start crying, which feels silly but I can’t seem to stop.\n\nAnd phone calls I used to handle easy, you know, calling people about church planning or checking details, I get all worked up over them now. I’ve even missed some planning things at church, and I never used to miss those. I’m just… not acting like myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: About a month now, I think. It started right after my close choir friend died… though time’s been feeling a little strange. I keep thinking of it by Sundays, because that’s when choir happens, and then I remember who used to sit where, and—well, yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain activities or places that remind you of your friend?\n\nPatient: I suppose I have, yes… not always on purpose. Ever since she passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. Church things especially feel different now. I’ll see the potluck list and start crying, and then I remember how she always knew who was bringing what—she was so organized, you know, the kind of person who’d bring extra napkins just in case.\n\nAnd then even making calls for planning, things I used to do without thinking, I get all worked up and put them off. I’ve missed some church planning things too, and I never would’ve done that before. It’s like anything connected to all of that just knocks me sideways.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific reminders of your friend that you intentionally try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always sit there and say, “I’m avoiding this,” but I do steer away from things that make me think of her. Like those potluck lists—she always knew exactly who was bringing what, and now I see one and I just start crying. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nAnd the calls for church planning, those used to be nothing for me, but now I get all worked up before I even pick up the phone. Then I’ll put it off, and sometimes I just don’t go to the planning things at all, which is not like me. Ever since she passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making an effort to avoid thinking about your friend or things that remind you of her?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. I don’t always mean to, but when something reminds me of her, I sort of turn away from it. Ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself.\n\nLike the potluck lists—I know that sounds silly, it’s just a list, but she used to be so on top of those things, and now I see one and I start crying. And then I get all worked up about calls I used to handle easily, church planning calls and that sort of thing. I’ve even missed some planning things I never would’ve missed before. It’s like I try not to think about her, but everything at church brings her right back.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe a little, but I’m not sure I’d call it “lost interest.” Some things just feel different lately. I’ll think about doing something and then get distracted, or I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums before they get all leggy—my neighbor always says I fuss over them too much.\n\nBut, um… activities I used to enjoy? I guess I’d have to think about that. I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you're not enjoying the things you used to, like choir or other hobbies, as much as you did before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Choir still means a lot to me… or it did—well, it does. It’s just hard to separate whether I’m not enjoying it or whether I’m just tired by the time I get there, you know?\n\nAnd hobbies… I still putter around the house. I was trying to sort some old music folders the other day and found a recipe card tucked in one—don’t ask me why, I’m always putting things in odd places. So I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy things anymore. I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in choir or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure I can say that clearly. Choir is still choir, you know? I’ve been doing it so long it’s almost part of the week, like taking the bins out or checking whether I left the porch light on.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m just not in the mood, but then I’ll get there and hear someone warming up and it feels familiar. Other times I start thinking about whether I remembered to bring the right folder, or if I need to pick up milk on the way home, and then I realize I haven’t really thought about whether I enjoyed it or not.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a straightforward way. It’s all a bit muddled.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling less pleasure when you are participating in choir or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve sat there in choir and thought, “Oh, I’m not enjoying this.” It’s more like… I’m doing it, and then my mind wanders to whether I turned the stove off or if I forgot someone’s birthday card.\n\nSometimes the singing feels nice, I suppose, but then someone will mention a hymn we used to do years ago and I start remembering all sorts of other things—like the old church hall with that awful coffee urn that always leaked. So I don’t know if it’s less pleasure exactly. I haven’t been measuring it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting less pleasure out of the things you do compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can compare it neatly, honestly. Before she passed… well, things were different, but I don’t always remember exactly how I felt doing things then versus now.\n\nSometimes I’ll be at choir and I’m more focused on whether I’m standing in the right place or if I brought my glasses—last week I had them on my head the whole time, which is silly—and then I think, “Was I enjoying that?” I’m not sure.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I know I’m getting less pleasure. I just haven’t really noticed it in that clear a way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad or empty most of the time exactly, but… ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself.\n\nI’ll be looking at the potluck list for church and suddenly I’m crying over who’s bringing coleslaw, which is ridiculous, really. And calls I used to make without thinking—just ordinary planning calls—I get all worked up about them now. I’ve even missed some church planning things I never would have missed before. I used to be the one reminding everyone else.\n\nSo… yes, something’s been off since then. It’s not always “empty,” maybe, but I’m not myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I still go to things, mostly. Choir, church bits and pieces… although I did miss that one meeting, but that was partly because I got the time mixed up, and then I couldn’t find the little notebook where I’d written it down. I’ve had that notebook for years, it’s got a blue cover—well, it used to be blue, now it’s sort of gray from being in my handbag.\n\nSo… lost interest? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought, “I don’t care about this anymore.” It’s more that I don’t always feel quite like myself when I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less interest in the things you do now compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t sat down and measured it against before, if that makes sense. I still turn up to things, or I try to—choir and church things and all that—though sometimes I’m a bit scattered about it.\n\nIt’s funny, I was looking for my choir folder the other day and found an old recipe card instead, my mother’s handwriting on it, and then I got completely sidetracked thinking I should make that casserole again. So… what was the question? Interest. Right.\n\nI don’t think I’ve clearly noticed having less interest, no. It’s more that I don’t always know how I feel while I’m doing things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “pulling away” sounds so deliberate, doesn’t it? Like I’m choosing not to answer the phone or not to see people. I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nI still talk to people, though sometimes I get mixed up about who I meant to call. My sister rang the other day and I was in the middle of sorting old church bulletins—why I kept so many, I couldn’t tell you—and then I forgot to call her back until later. But I don’t think I’m trying to keep away from anyone.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed pulling away, no. Not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social gatherings or events since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I still mean to go. I’ll have the date in my head and then the potluck list comes around, and I just start crying over whether we need coleslaw or rolls, which is ridiculous, isn’t it?\n\nSince my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t quite been myself. Things like church planning meetings, calls I used to make without thinking—now I get all worked up, and I’ve missed things I really wouldn’t have missed before. Not because I don’t care. I just… I don’t seem to manage it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself wanting to be alone more often since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like my quiet time anyway—always have, really. My husband used to say I could spend a whole afternoon rearranging a drawer and be perfectly content, though that’s not quite fair.\n\nSince she passed… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m home and I think, “Oh, I should call someone,” and then I end up watering the plants or looking for a bill I already paid. But I haven’t really noticed myself wanting to be alone more, not clearly. It’s more that things sort of get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy or look forward to activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I still like things when I get there, if that makes sense. Like choir—well, not quite the same lately, of course, but I wouldn’t say I don’t enjoy anything anymore.\n\nSometimes I think I’m looking forward to something, and then I get distracted by some little chore. Yesterday I found myself cleaning out a recipe box, and there were three different cards for banana bread—why anyone needs three, I don’t know. My mother’s handwriting was on one of them, and then I sat there longer than I meant to.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it’s that I no longer enjoy things. I just get a bit tangled up getting to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often, exactly. I mean, she comes to mind, sure—especially with choir things, or if I see something she would’ve had an opinion about. She had very particular opinions about tablecloths, of all things.\n\nBut I don’t sit there all day thinking about her, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… little reminders pop up, and then I get carried off for a bit. I’m not sure how much is normal, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like a part of you has died along with your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that sounds very dramatic when you put it that way. I haven’t sat down and measured it against before, if that makes sense. I still turn up to things, or I try to—choir and church things and all that—though sometimes I’m a bit scattered about it.\n\nIt’s funny, I was looking for my choir folder the other day and found an old recipe card instead, my mother’s handwriting on it, and then I got completely sidetracked thinking I should make that casserole again. So… what was the question? Interest. Right.\n\nI don’t think I’ve clearly noticed having less interest, no. It’s more that I don’t always know how I feel while I’m doing things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “pulling away” sounds so deliberate, doesn’t it? Like I’m choosing not to answer the phone or not to see people. I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nI still talk to people, though sometimes I get mixed up about who I meant to call. My sister rang the other day and I was in the middle of sorting old church bulletins—why I kept so many, I couldn’t tell you—and then I forgot to call her back until later. But I don’t think I’m trying to keep away from anyone.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed pulling away, no. Not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social gatherings or events since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I still mean to go. I’ll have the date in my head and then the potluck list comes around, and I just start crying over whether we need coleslaw or rolls, which is ridiculous, isn’t it?\n\nSince my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t quite been myself. Things like church planning meetings, calls I used to make without thinking—now I get all worked up, and I’ve missed things I really wouldn’t have missed before. Not because I don’t care. I just… I don’t seem to manage it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself wanting to be alone more often since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like my quiet time anyway—always have, really. My husband used to say I could spend a whole afternoon rearranging a drawer and be perfectly content, though that’s not quite fair.\n\nSince she passed… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m home and I think, “Oh, I should call someone,” and then I end up watering the plants or looking for a bill I already paid. But I haven’t really noticed myself wanting to be alone more, not clearly. It’s more that things sort of get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy or look forward to activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I still like things when I get there, if that makes sense. Like choir—well, not quite the same lately, of course, but I wouldn’t say I don’t enjoy anything anymore.\n\nSometimes I think I’m looking forward to something, and then I get distracted by some little chore. Yesterday I found myself cleaning out a recipe box, and there were three different cards for banana bread—why anyone needs three, I don’t know. My mother’s handwriting was on one of them, and then I sat there longer than I meant to.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it’s that I no longer enjoy things. I just get a bit tangled up getting to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often, exactly. I mean, she comes to mind, sure—especially with choir things, or if I see something she would’ve had an opinion about. She had very particular opinions about tablecloths, of all things.\n\nBut I don’t sit there all day thinking about her, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… little reminders pop up, and then I get carried off for a bit. I’m not sure how much is normal, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like a part of you has died along with your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that sounds very dramatic when you put it that way. I wouldn’t say a part of me died, no. I miss her, of course, and there are these odd little gaps—like when I see something funny and think, oh, she’d appreciate that.\n\nBut “part of me died”… I’m not sure. It’s more like the room is arranged differently now, if that makes any sense. Like when you move a chair and keep bumping into where it used to be. My sister did that once with a coffee table, and I had a bruise for a week—anyway. No, I don’t think I’d put it that way exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the sadness about your friend's passing is still as intense as it was initially?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to measure that, really. At first it was… well, everything felt very sharp, I suppose. Now it comes and goes more, maybe? Some days I think I’m doing all right, and then something small happens—like I’ll see a hymnal left open, or I’ll hear someone laugh in a way that’s almost like her, and there it is again.\n\nBut “as intense as initially”… I don’t know. It’s different, I think. Not gone. Just different. My neighbor once said grief is like weather, which I thought was a bit much at the time, but I suppose there’s something to it. Some days it’s just cloudy, and other days you didn’t bring an umbrella."
  }
